# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-02

## RAG

| **[Decompose, Estimate, Select: Coverage-Aware Context Construction for Long-Form RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40314v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds a language model's output in retrieved evidence, but for complex questions spanning multiple aspects of an information need, relevance-based retrieval may return redundant evidence while leaving some aspects unsupported. We propose a coverage-aware retrieval pipeline that decomposes queries into subquestions, ranks candidates with a coverage-trained bi-encoder, predicts a document\,$\times$\,subquestion coverage matrix with a cross-encoder, and selects documents by greedy maximum-marginal coverage. On TREC RAG 2024, our approach improves $α$-nDCG@10, which rewards newly covered nuggets and discounts redundant evidence, from $0.625$ to $0.702$, approaching the $0.718$ gold-nugget upper bound without sacrificing relevance. We then find that ground-truth oracle retrieval, despite substantially higher retrieval coverage, delivers no more nugget coverage to the answer than our estimated selection. This suggests that answer completeness depends not only on what evidence is retrieved, but also on how effectively the generator uses it. To address this, we introduce a \emph{grounded self-feedback loop} in which the model identifies underused evidence from its first answer and generates again without gold supervision, raising $V_{\mathrm{strict}}$, the fraction of vital reference nuggets fully supported by the answer, by $0.086$ over two passes while largely preserving faithfulness.</p></details> |  |
| **[TCC-RAG: Temporal Causal Chain-Guided RAG for Temporal Knowledge Graph Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38366v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have spurred growing interest in temporal knowledge graph question answering (TKGQA). Most existing methods rely on semantic retrieval augmented generation (RAG), which suffers from fundamental limitations in complex temporal reasoning. On the one hand, semantic-based RAG methods struggle to capture the multi-hop causal knowledge required for complex temporal reasoning. On the other hand, these methods introduce excessive redundant knowledge, trapping LLMs in spurious reasoning chains that potentially result in erroneous answers. To address these problems, we propose a Temporal Causal Chain-guided RAG (TCC-RAG) to retrieve a concise yet sufficient set of causal knowledge to support complex temporal reasoning. Specifically, we reformulate the temporal knowledge graph into a multi-view causal cue graph, with causal cues inducing connections that explicitly encode temporal evolution and causal dynamics. Based on the causal cue graph, we construct a query-specific temporal causal chain to guide the retrieval process, enabling the model to focus on a concise set of causally relevant knowledge. Finally, we introduce a causal evidence filter to refine the retrieved knowledge, ensuring its causal consistency with the underlying temporal logic of the query. Experiments show that TCC-RAG achieves substantial performance gains of 10.72% and 8.40% on two TKGQA benchmarks, and delivers a remarkable 62.97% improvement for multi-step temporal reasoning.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 6 figures, ICDE2027 Research Track</p></details> |
| **[DeFT-RAG: Incrementally Decomposing Multi-Hop Questions into Self-Contained Sub-Questions with Reusable Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37826v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) lets a large language model (LLM) answer questions by retrieving relevant passages from a large corpus. However, RAG struggles with multi-hop questions, which require chaining facts spread across several documents through intermediate entities that the question never mentions. Graph-based RAG methods retrieve once from the entities a question names and so rarely recover the full evidence chain. Iterative RAG methods recover it step by step, but their accuracy is limited by the many tasks they ask the LLM to perform, such as planning the sub-questions, deciding their dependencies, rewriting them as answers arrive, and keeping track of what is known. We propose DeFT-RAG, a RAG framework built on three components. First, DeFT-RAG generates one self-contained sub-question at a time from the original question and the sub-questions answered so far with their answers (QA pairs). Each sub-question names every entity it asks about, which removes the need to plan, track dependencies or rewrite, and lets the LLM focus on a single fine-grained task at a time. Second, DeFT-RAG moves the remaining LLM work outside the LLM: rule-based updates to a QA table that keeps track of what is known, and yes/no decisions made by a fine-tuned lightweight decision model. Third, DeFT-RAG caches the QA pairs and reuses an answer when a later question asks a sub-question with the same meaning. On MuSiQue, 2WikiMultiHopQA and HotpotQA, DeFT-RAG achieves the best accuracy among thirteen baselines with both gpt-4o-mini and Qwen3.6 as the backbone, while giving the LLM fewer input tokens than any other iterative method. With a QA cache shared across 1,000 MuSiQue questions, 23.1% of sub-questions are answered from the cache, which reduces LLM calls by 12.9% without lowering accuracy.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 2 figures, 7 tables. Code: https://github.com/Dingyi-Kang/DeFT-RAG</p></details> |
| **[When Is Additional Retrieval Worthwhile? A Cost-Aware Study of Evidence Gain and Answer Reliability in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37358v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can benefit from more sophisticated retrieval, but extra retrieval may be unnecessary for many questions and gains in evidence retrieval may not translate into faithful or correct answers. We study a cost-aware, two-action retrieval policy that chooses between a dense-retrieval-plus-reranking baseline and a hybrid lexical--dense retriever. Rather than classifying baseline retrieval as simply successful or unsuccessful, we estimate the question-specific incremental evidence-retrieval benefit of hybrid retrieval using features available before the routing decision. A regularized linear model and decision threshold are selected using document-grouped cross-validation and frozen prior to held-out evaluation. On 60 previously unseen QASPER training-split articles (188 questions, 169 evidence-evaluable), the policy triggers hybrid retrieval on 46 questions (24.5\%) and obtains the same evidence Hit@10 as always-hybrid retrieval (78.70\%, versus 76.92\% for always-keep). Paired, paper-cluster bootstrap intervals do not establish a reliable utility advantage. We further compare generated answers from a 4-bit Qwen3-4B model under the three retrieval policies. Provisional answer-level assessments show no clear correctness or faithfulness improvement after partial review, despite the evidence-retrieval result. The generation assessments remain model-assisted and lack independent human validation. These results expose an important distinction between retrieving relevant evidence efficiently and improving the reliability of the generated answer. The work is presented as a reproducible empirical study, not a claim of statistically established answer-quality superiority.</p></details> |  |
| **[p-DCIM: A 28nm Probabilistic SRAM DCIM based Ising Machine for Near-Memory Context Selection in LLM-RAG Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37033v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present a fabricated SRAM DCIM Ising machine that performs set-level context selection for large language model (LLM) with retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). In RAG, the evidence-selection stage is increasingly delegated to repeated language-model reranking, which adds substantial energy to the inference pipeline. We reformulate evidence selection as a quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) problem and solve it on a dual-mode digital compute-in-memory (DCIM) macro: the same multiply-and-accumulate datapath computes the embedding-similarity scores that populate the QUBO coefficients and evaluates the per-spin Hamiltonian differences that drive the anneal. The annealing temperature is set by a digital plane-count control along with the SRAM pseudo-read random bit flips, giving graded cooling entirely from on-chip digital configuration. The macro is taped-out in a 28 nm process [0.16 mm2, 0.41 mW] and is proposed to be integrated as part of the logic base-die in hybrid high-bandwidth memory (HBM) and high-bandwidth flash (HBF) stacks in a GPU package, where only the selected passage identifiers return to the host. Replacing the language-model selection pass with the near-memory Ising solver yields a macro normalized selection-energy estimate more than six orders of magnitude lower than the LLM-based baseline and reduces the evaluated total energy per query by 67%, while maintaining comparable token-level F1 on the evaluated examples.</p></details> |  |
| **[RAG-CSI: Global Local Retrieval via Joint State and Dynamics Matching for CSI Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36881v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Channel state information (CSI) prediction forecasts future channel states from historical observations, supporting transceiver design and resource allocation with reduced measurement overhead. However, existing deep learning predictors mainly encode channel knowledge in model parameters, which can limit their ability to represent diverse patterns of channel dynamics across scenes. In this paper, we propose RAG-CSI, a retrieval-enhanced Transformer framework that augments prediction with relevant historical channel knowledge. Specifically, we construct a global--local CSI knowledge store containing representative observations across scenes and historical observations from the current serving scene. We further develop joint state--dynamics retrieval (JSDR), which jointly characterizes channel states, phase dynamics, and amplitude dynamics to retrieve relevant CSI sequences. A Transformer predictor then uses cross-attention to fuse the retrieved knowledge with the current CSI history and predict future CSI with limited additional model parameters. Experiments on a multi-scene CSI dataset show overall improvements exceeding 10\% across all five evaluation metrics over the best baseline for each metric, including a 15.42\% reduction in normalized mean squared error.</p></details> |  |
| **[Governed Sovereign RAG for Public Administration: Tripartite Epistemic Routing, Facet-Guided Clarification, and Deterministic Numeric Verification in Citizen Services](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36561v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deploying conversational assistants at government service desks faces a tension between regulatory certainty (zero tolerance for invented fees), data sovereignty prohibiting commercial clouds, and high rates of underspecified or out-of-jurisdiction citizen queries. We present and evaluate a governed, sovereign Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) architecture deployed on the official portal of the State of Tlaxcala, Mexico (tlaxcaladigital.gob.mx), covering 1,143 procedures and a decoupled directory of 924 offices. The system governs interactions via three pillars: (1) a two-stage tripartite epistemic gate (clarify_policy and a Boltzmann distribution over AnyJev) arbitrating among answering (SPECIFIC), facet-guided clarification (CLARIFY), and abstaining (ABSTAIN); (2) a post-generation deterministic numeric verifier (verify_numeric_guardrail) with SHA-256 cache invalidation ensuring every generated fee or validity figure belongs to the retrieved official record; and (3) an empirical evaluation across 380 organic production traces (8 weeks) and a 200-query stratified benchmark (5-fold CV: 86.41% +/- 7.50%). In production, 89.47% of traffic is resolved at Level 0 via deterministic templates without free generation (global p50 = 0.665 s; direct L0 p50 = 0.445 s; slow-tail p95 = 30.412 s under CPU queuing), with 0/40 fiscal amount hallucinations observed in Level 1 (95% bound <= 8.81%). On the benchmark, the system achieves 86.50% strict Top-1 accuracy (+10.00 pp over a binary neural gate, p = 0.00453, p_Holm = 0.01172), pairing a 93.00% conditional safe upper bound with a 70.87% dropout-adjusted effective resolution after accounting for 53.33% citizen drop-off in clarification menus.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 5 figures, 6 tables. Deployed in production at SEDECO/CEMERT, State of Tlaxcala, Mexico</p></details> |
| **[QESChunker: A Single Objective Unifies Overlapping and Non-Overlapping Chunking for RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36557v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chunking is a fundamental preprocessing step in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), yet most existing chunkers rely primarily on heuristics. We introduce the Question--Evidence-Supervised Chunker (QESChunker), to our knowledge the first method for constructing static chunk indices from question--evidence supervision. QESChunker constructs document-specific chunk indices by optimizing over human-annotated or LLM-generated question--evidence pairs, without access to held-out test queries. We formulate non-overlapping and overlapping index construction through a unified question--evidence utility-maximization objective, turning chunking into a supervised combinatorial optimization problem. Under the non-overlap constraint, the objective decomposes over chunks and can be optimized exactly in polynomial time by dynamic programming. When overlap is allowed, the objective becomes monotone submodular; exact optimization is NP-hard, but our greedy achieves a $(1-1/\sqrt{e})$-approximation. Across two self-constructed and three public datasets, both variants outperform existing chunking methods in complete-evidence recall in nearly all evaluated settings, under the same overlap regime and a fixed hybrid retriever. These results recast chunking as supervised, budget-aware index construction for evidence retrieval.</p></details> | <details><summary>16 pa...</summary><p>16 pages, 8 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |
| **[ScalableRAG: High-Quality RAG with Exact Corpus Operations at Minimal Ingestion Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25135v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>RAG systems face a capability-cost gap: lightweight retrievers locate evidence but expose no operands for exact corpus-level operations (counts, intersections, aggregations), while knowledge ingestion systems obtain them only through expensive ingestion. ScalableRAG closes this gap with a persistent typed-set workspace: an agent builds named document and value sets at query time and composes them through set algebra, typed extraction, aggregation, and diagnostics. Zero-Ingestion ScalableRAG needs no preprocessing, not even a vector index. It matches the leading baseline on four conventional QA benchmarks (74.75 versus 74.64) and on aggregative Hotels-50 beats every evaluated system, scoring 71.74: 36.23 above the leading baseline (SRAG), and 40.22 points above A-RAG. Removing the workspace drops Hotels-50 to 38.4. On Hotels-350, the partition SRAG itself reports on, ScalableRAG leads SRAG by 9.3 points. Limited-Ingestion ScalableRAG adds a lightweight index and is the only evaluated system above 80 judge score at 5M documents, at 13 to 16 seconds per question and under 20 minutes ingestion; at 1M it scores 84.5 to A-RAG's 80.0 with 16x lower latency and 0.5% of its ingestion time. ScalableRAG combines minimal ingestion, conventional-QA parity, exact corpus operations, and million-document viability; no evaluated alternative provides all four. Our code is available at https://github.com/cohesity/ScalableRAG .</p></details> |  |
| **[OA-RAG: Ontology-based Agentic RAG for Faithful LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36335v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) perform well across many real-world tasks but remain prone to hallucination, limiting their reliability in domains such as biomedicine and healthcare, where answers must be grounded in established knowledge. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) mitigates this problem by incorporating external information, but often overlooks logical relationships and relies heavily on the LLM's own reasoning, which can compromise answer faithfulness. We propose OA-RAG, an agent that integrates formal reasoning with LLM reasoning, drawing on both the logical structure and informal annotations of domain ontologies. For each answer, OA-RAG reports the ontology observations used as evidence and a model-assessed grounding level (entailed, supported, related, or unrelated) that characterises the strength of that evidence. Across benchmarks derived from three large-scale ontologies and six real-world medical question-answering tasks, OA-RAG achieves the best overall performance among the evaluated ontology-fine-tuned and popular RAG methods, with particularly strong results on reasoning-intensive tasks. The reported grounding levels also offer a useful indication of answer reliability, with stronger grounding generally associated with higher accuracy. Finally, our results demonstrate the value of ontologies as knowledge sources: OA-RAG is competitive with, and sometimes outperforms, domain-specific RAG systems that draw on sources such as textbooks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Geometric Gain Graph: Zero-Token Graph Construction for Multi-Hop RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19622v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems struggle with multi-hop reasoning due to a fundamental inability to balance relevance and novelty. Traditional dense retrievers are constrained by surface-level matching, falling into a homogenized "Similarity Trap". Conversely, emerging Graph RAGs attempt to explore novel documents via heuristic entity extraction, but they introduce prohibitive Large Language Model (LLM) overhead and easily drift into off-topic "Novelty Traps". To fundamentally resolve this contradiction, we propose Geometric Gain Graph (G$^3$RAG), the novel zero-token graph construction paradigm for RAG. G$^3$RAG completely discards the expensive heuristic extraction by LLMs, aiming instead to directly quantify the information gain between nodes through the native document feature space. To this end, we designed a concise edge weight criterion, $\cosθ\cdot \sinθ$, which naturally maps the trade-off between relevance and novelty into a geometric measurement of directional consistency and orthogonality in the vector space, thereby driving the token-free topological construction of the graph. Furthermore, we introduce a topological penalty to suppress the excessive connectivity of high-frequency hubs, forcing the transient diffusion process toward long-tail peripheral nodes that carry critical indirect evidence. Extensive experiments demonstrate that, while entirely eliminating LLM token costs during graph construction, G$^3$RAG significantly outperforms state-of-the-art Graph RAG baselines on complex multi-hop QA benchmarks. The code is available at: https://github.com/deeplearning-LI/G3RAG</p></details> |  |
| **[Reading Beyond OCR Noise: Robust Cross-Lingual RAG for Legislative Archives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35240v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>India's historical public archives present severe computer vision and document image analysis challenges: decades of parliamentary records survive only as degraded, multi-column scanned pages featuring mixed scripts, complex layouts, and dense visual noise where OCR failure is the primary obstacle to information retrieval. We present an end-to-end framework that couples vision-based layout segmentation and multi-script OCR with an agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline. Our approach specifically addresses how visual degradation and layout fragmentation propagate into text extraction errors, using an iterative agentic loop to navigate around OCR noise and layout ambiguities before generating answers. Evaluated on the 200,000-page Punjab Vidhan Sabha archive, our system demonstrates robust handling of historical document degradation, outperforming standard text-only baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[CalibraRL: Confidence-Calibrated Reinforcement Learning for Mitigating Hallucinations in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35142v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) often produce plausible but incorrect answers, motivating reinforcement learning methods that reward correct answers, penalize incorrect answers, and allow abstention. However, fixed outcome-based rewards do not distinguish incorrect responses with different confidence levels or account for differences in answering reliability across queries. Consequently, such rewards may inadequately penalize overconfident errors and fail to discourage unnecessary abstention. We propose CalibraRL, a reinforcement learning framework that incorporates response confidence and query-level answering evidence into reward design. For non-abstaining responses, CalibraRL combines correctness-based utility with a Brier-based penalty that encourages agreement between confidence and correctness. For abstentions, it estimates conditional answering reliability using a Beta posterior derived from the correctness of non-abstaining responses within each sampled group. The abstention reward increases with the posterior probability that the model's accuracy on non-abstaining responses is below a predefined threshold. CalibraRL reuses training rollouts without requiring additional annotations indicating whether the model knows the answer to each question. Experiments on three QA datasets demonstrate that CalibraRL improves answer precision and reduces hallucinations compared with strong baselines. Further analysis shows that CalibraRL reduces mean expected calibration error (ECE) by 30.8\% across two backbone models and three datasets, highlighting its potential to improve the reliability of retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) in web search.</p></details> | 12 pages, 10 figures |
| **[Hallucination-Aware Multi-Agent RAG via Internal Attention Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35056v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds language models in retrieved evidence, but its outputs often contain claims unsupported by that evidence. Recent multi-agent RAG systems decompose the pipeline into cooperative decisions, yet they typically optimize all agents under a shared task reward such as EM or F1, which measures answer overlap rather than faithfulness to evidence. We propose a hallucination-aware multi-agent RAG framework that combines the task reward with an internal hallucination-risk signal computed from the generator's attention-head norms. Three role-specific LoRA adapters, a query planner, an evidence selector, and an answer generator, share a frozen backbone. A lightweight critic is trained once on TruthfulQA truthful/false pairs and then kept frozen, providing a complementary risk score that is added to the shared reward during cooperative PPO training. Across three backbones (Qwen3-8B, Llama3-8B, Llama2-13B) and three benchmarks (RAGTruth, HotpotQA, 2WikiMultiHopQA), our method improves task performance and reduces hallucination rates over the strongest multi-agent baseline. Ablations and critic diagnostics show that the TruthfulQA-calibrated signal provides useful reward shaping that transfers across RAG tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Findi...</summary><p>Findings of the Association for Computational Linguistics: EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[Adaptive Retrieval, Selective Answers: Evaluating Conformal Factuality in Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34872v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can improve factual grounding without guaranteeing that every generated claim is supported. Previous work has studied claim-level conformal factuality in single-stage RAG and, more recently, multi-hop RAG. We examine its behavior in agentic RAG, where a language-model policy adaptively issues follow-up retrieval queries or terminates retrieval before response generation. We retain the retrieval-based relevance-scoring formulation and post-generation conformal filtering and evaluate six model-dataset configurations: GPT-6 Luna and Llama 3.1 8B on HotpotQA, Natural Questions, and TriviaQA. Each configuration uses 60 fixed questions, 100 seeded 30/30 calibration-test partitions, five conformal target levels, and a no-conformal-prediction baseline. Across the six configurations, higher target levels are accompanied by higher observed full-support rates and reduced answer availability. At the 95% target, non-empty response coverage ranges from 17.23% to 60.17%, claim retention from 10.72% to 43.16%, and full support among non-empty outputs from 89.14% to 95.56%. We formulate the adaptive retrieval trajectory explicitly and characterize the reliability-informativeness trade-off when conformal factuality is applied after agent termination.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 1 figure, 5 tables. Code: https://github.com/aimalrehman92/conformal-rag-agentic</p></details> |
| **[Logic Hijacking of RAG: Single-Document Poisoning via Local Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34829v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Single-document corpus poisoning under black-box access poses a severe threat to Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). However, existing attacks predominantly rely on explicit factual refutations or imperative instruction overrides. Modern aligned LLMs often arbitrate such conflicts conservatively, rejecting overt contradictions in favor of parametric memory or clean retrieved context while triggering input guardrails. We propose MONOLOGUE, a single-document poisoning framework based on Logic Hijacking without explicit refutation. Rather than disputing the ground truth, our method concedes it as an authoritative historical baseline before introducing the update, weaving a grounded record-correction narrative (e.g., dated institutional reattributions) that presents the counterfactual target as the most recent update. To prevent suspicious live writes, payloads are refined entirely offline via local multi-dimensional gated optimization, coupling automated text quality audits with surrogate behavioral verification. Evaluations across three benchmarks, three retrieval backends, and four open-weight generator regimes show that our method achieves the strongest overall attack efficacy among the evaluated single-document poisoning attacks.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 3 figures, 14 tables</p></details> |
| **[Can System-1-Style Decision Models Replace Dedicated Rerankers in RAG? A Systems Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34816v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems often use a dedicated reranker to choose which retrieved candidates reach the language model. Reranking improves relevance but adds latency, so teams look for faster alternatives. System-1style decision models make direct, structured predictions instead of generating long responses. Could one provide comparable reranking quality with less inference overhead? We first screen 21 open-weight models on 11 benchmarks, then compare a promising decision model with a dedicated reranker on identical candidate lists. The default decision-model setup fails on many long candidate sets, so scoring only the requests it completes gives a misleading ranking. Limiting candidate text length largely restores reliability and makes some configurations competitive. A frozen evaluation on new retrieval data supports the reliability and ranking results, but confirms the latency requirement for only one of the two selected configurations. Across workloads, which model ranks better changes, and a tuned reranker remains faster and uses far less GPU memory. A decision model can perform competitive reranking, but replacing a reranker requires weighing quality, latency and memory together, and teams should tune the reranker they already run first.</p></details> |  |
| **[LatentRAG: Latent Reasoning and Retrieval for Efficient Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06285v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Single-step retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) provides an efficient way to incorporate external information for simple question answering tasks but struggles with complex questions. Agentic RAG extends this paradigm by replacing single-step retrieval with a multi-step process, in which the large language model (LLM) acts as a search agent that generates intermediate thoughts and subqueries to iteratively interact with the retrieval system. This iterative process incurs substantial latency due to the autoregressive generation of lengthy thoughts and subqueries. To address this limitation, we propose LatentRAG, a novel framework that shifts both reasoning and retrieval from discrete language space to continuous latent space. Unlike existing explicit methods that generate natural language thoughts or subqueries token-by-token, LatentRAG produces latent tokens for thoughts and subqueries directly from the hidden states in a single forward pass. We align LLMs with dense retrieval models in the latent space, enabling retrieval over latent subquery tokens and supporting end-to-end joint optimization. To improve transparency and encourage semantically meaningful latent representations, we incorporate a parallel latent decoding mechanism that translates latent tokens back into natural language. Extensive experiments on seven benchmark datasets show that LatentRAG achieves performance comparable to explicit agentic RAG methods while reducing inference latency by approximately 90%, substantially narrowing the latency gap with traditional single-step RAG.</p></details> | <details><summary>Neura...</summary><p>Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS), 2026</p></details> |
| **[CoinRAG: Contextualized Information Nugget KV Cache Reuse for Long-Context RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07458v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent optimization studies on Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) have exploited chunk-level KV cache reuse to avoid processing long retrieved contexts for higher efficiency, while significant information redundancy and noise still remain in the coarse-grained chunks. This paper optimizes the Pareto frontier under low prefill latency constraints while maximizing accuracy by proposing CoinRAG (Contextualized Information Nugget KV Cache Reuse for Long-Context RAG). The name metaphorically reflects our core mechanism: much like assembling small tokens (or "coins") to accumulate a larger value, CoinRAG compositionally reuses offline-computed, fine-grained nugget caches to form a learned contextual representation efficiently in a more semantically relevant but compact manner. Specifically, instead of full-chunk encoding, CoinRAG identifies query-relevant semantic units within retrieved chunks through two-stage retrieval and seamlessly assembles their sliced KV representations with a chunk-level context. CoinRAG can be integrated with CacheBlend's selective recomputation for further accuracy gains under the same latency budget. Extensive evaluations on LongBench multi-hop question answering tasks demonstrate that CoinRAG substantially shortens the active context and outperforms the other baselines with a new Pareto frontier and an average 5.3% relative improvement in answer quality (F1) under a standard fast prefill latency budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[GEMS-RAG: Graph-grounded Engineering Multimodal Standards Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33277v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Engineering standards are challenging knowledge sources for large language models (LLMs) because they are hierarchical, multimodal, and densely connected through figures, tables, cross-references, and normative rules. Existing retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) methods often rely on unstructured text retrieval and struggle with questions requiring joint reasoning over textual provisions, visual specifications, and document structure. We propose GEMS-RAG (Graph-grounded Engineering Multimodal Standards Retrieval-Augmented Generation), a framework for grounded reasoning over engineering standards. GEMS-RAG combines hierarchical parsing, fine-grained visual entity extraction, multimodal knowledge graph construction, and graph-grounded hybrid retrieval. Instead of treating figures as monolithic images, it decomposes them into meaningful visual entities linked to textual provisions, engineering concepts, and regulatory rules. During inference, dense, sparse, and visual retrieval are combined with graph expansion to identify relevant evidence across text, figures, and document structure. We evaluate GEMS-RAG on the Manual on Uniform Traffic Control Devices (MUTCD), a large engineering standard containing egulations, technical drawings, and standardized traffic-control devices. Results show that graph-grounded multimodal retrieval improves retrieval effectiveness and question-answering performance over text-only, multimodal, and graph-based RAG baselines. Beyond MUTCD, GEMS-RAG offers a general framework for reasoning over engineering codes, technical manuals, regulatory documents, and scientific standards in safety-critical applications.</p></details> |  |
| **[From One Query to Many: A Critical Survey of Privacy Accounting in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21245v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) enables large language models to leverage external knowledge but introduces privacy risks when the retrieved corpus contains sensitive information. Differential privacy (DP) offers formal protection, yet interpreting and comparing privacy guarantees across existing DP-RAG methods remains challenging. In particular, a privacy budget reported for a single response does not directly characterize privacy loss over repeated queries, and even identical values of $\varepsilon$ may represent different guarantees depending on the protected data unit, accounting horizon, and observable outputs. This survey critically examines how privacy accounting evolves from single-query generation to multi-query RAG systems. We categorize existing approaches into query-time privatization, multi-query accounting, and one-time corpus privatization. We analyze their underlying assumptions and privacy--utility trade-offs. Rather than ranking methods by $\varepsilon$ alone, we propose a framework that interprets a DP-RAG guarantee along three dimensions: privacy granularity, accounting scope, and release boundaries. | **[REAP: Enhancing RAG with Recursive Evaluation and Adaptive Planning for Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.09966v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has been extensively employed to mitigate hallucinations in large language models (LLMs). Insufficient exploitation of retrieved content and the neglect of latent clues fail to ensure the accuracy of reasoning outcomes. To overcome these limitations, we propose Recursive Evaluation and Adaptive Planning (REAP), whose core idea is to explicitly maintain structured sub-tasks and facts related to the current task through the Sub-task Planner (SP) and Fact Extractor (FE) modules. SP maintains a global perspective, guiding the overall reasoning direction and evaluating the task state based on the outcomes of FE, enabling dynamic optimization of the task-solving trajectory. FE performs fine-grained analysis over retrieved content to extract reliable answers and clues. These two modules incrementally enrich a logically coherent representation of global knowledge, enhancing the reliability and the traceability of the reasoning process. Furthermore, we propose a unified task paradigm design that enables effective multi-task fine-tuning, significantly enhancing SP's performance on complex, data-scarce tasks. We conduct extensive experiments on multiple public multi-hop datasets, and the results demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms existing RAG methods in both in-domain and out-of-domain settings, validating its effectiveness in complex multi-hop reasoning tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>publi...</summary><p>published in AAAI 2026</p></details> |
| **[Upgrade the Retriever First: What an LLM Call per Chunk Buys in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23533v4)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many RAG pipelines pay one LLM call per chunk at indexing time to add titles, summaries, keywords and questions (enrichment) or document context (contextual retrieval, CR). Under a plan made public before any index was built, 7B enrichment beats a free section-path prefix under a hybrid retriever by +5.5 recall points on Qasper; on FreshStack its gain (+2.7) does not survive Holm adjustment. It is equivalent to CR, which sends 5.8-8.7 times the input tokens, and Qasper answer F1 (7B reader) is equivalent for both contrasts. Header-led chunks beat 512-character windows; on FreshStack they lose to overlapping 256-token windows. | **[RIT-RAG: Navigating Document Corpora with Retrieval-Induced Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11370v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds language models in external corpora. Across financial, scientific, and customer-support benchmarks, RIT-RAG achieves the highest answer accuracy among vanilla, graph-based, and agentic baselines. | **[Poisoning the Neighborhood: A Topology-Aware Topic-Level Attack on Large-Scale HNSW-based RAG Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21464v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) mitigates hallucinations and knowledge staleness in large language models using external knowledge. | **[Copying Is Not Grounding: Training Faithful Long-Form RAG with Authored-Sentence Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18442v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In search engines and assistants, long-form RAG answers can mislead readers with claims their sources do not support. A sentence copied from the passages is supported almost by construction, so trained models learn to copy rather than ground what they write. Common defenses only move the hack: a KL or copy penalty leaves copying in place or trades it for shorter, emptier answers and refusals. GRPO, the standard RL algorithm, makes it worse by giving every sentence its answer's advantage, reinforcing copied and grounded sentences alike. We propose Authored-Sentence Credit (ASC) , which fixes both by moving GRPO's reward and advantage from the answer to the sentence level. The reward credits only sentences that are authored rather than copied, say more than the question, and are supported by the passages. The advantage compares each sentence with every sentence sampled for the question, so one that earns no credit ranks last and is never reinforced. Across four models and two in-domain, three held-out corpora, only ASC lowers the error rate of authored sentences and writes more supported ones in every setting, by 38% and 46% at the median under held-out evaluators, giving readers more grounded information. We conclude that a faithfulness reward should credit what a model writes, not what it copies.</p></details> |  |

To address this issue, one promising approach is to reuse intermediate computational results across different queries to eliminate redundant computation. In addition, PerCache can adapt its configurations to dynamic system loads, aiming to maximize caching utility with constrained resource consumption. EvoRAG introduces a feedback-driven backpropagation mechanism that attributes feedback to retrieved paths by measuring their utility for response and propagates this utility back to individual triplets, supporting fine-grained KG refinements towards more adaptive and accurate reasoning. Through EvoRAG, we establish a closed loop that couples feedback, LLM, and graph data, continuously enhancing the performance and robustness in real-world scenarios. Experimental results show that EvoRAG improves reasoning accuracy by $7.34\%$ over state-of-the-art KG-RAG frameworks. | **[Fast and Accurate: Improving Personalized LLM Performance by Semantic Concentration Inspired Workload Split between RAG and Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15921v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With ever increasing personalized large language model (LLM) services, the next question to answer is how to improve the quality of services/users' experiences, which is indicated by personalized LLM generation accuracy/quality and inference latency. | **[OntoAR-HAR: Ontology-Guided Agentic RAG with Personalized Memory for Human Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15292v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs increasingly support sensor-based human activity recognition (HAR) by linking motion signals with activity semantics and physical knowledge. Its tree-structured activity ontology decomposes classification into local decisions among semantically related activities. The static store supplies node-constrained examples, while offline-discovered executable signal features provide numerical evidence for local distinctions. During inference, the personalized store collects selected user observations, which the LLM integrates with static knowledge and signal features while traversing the ontology to an activity label. We observe that the honest nodes are the same in both steps. | **[From extreme weather to agricultural consequences with agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40641v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extreme weather is forecast days ahead, yet its consequences for agriculture remain hard to predict: a forecast says where a storm will strike, not which crops will suffer or how much is at risk, and the records that could tell span many sources. | **[Agentic process evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40071v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The way software gets built is changing dramatically. AI agents are autonomous team members, but unlike human engineers, they are ephemeral - their reasoning, decisions, and tool calls vanish the moment the session ends. Modern Security incidents involving intent mismatch or injection are becoming increasingly common - forcing us to address AI Agents as an insider threat. No widely adopted standard yet enables agentic provenance and governance and its obvious that you can't hold an ephemeral process accountable. The git log was designed for human commits. It shows what changed. It does not show what an AI agent did, which model it used, which tools it called, or whether anyone was accountable for the outcome. Every governance framework, every audit trail, every compliance control in the SDLC was built around one assumption: a human made the decision. That assumption no longer holds. This shift introduces 3 main procedural problems (not addressing scale) - Supply-chain vector - The agentic coder is software, so it is a supply-chain security risk. Policy guardrails - How to ensure the agentic coder is compliant with the policies of the organization. Task-alignment - How to align the agentic coder on its tasks and goals. We introduce Agentic Process Evidence (APE), a signed, tamper-evident, machine-readable attestation of agentic processes, linking the sessions to their outcome (process-aware logging). APE enables Policy-as-Code control over the agentic processes, allowing for human in the loop only when it's needed. Twenty-three of 24 runs respected the position limit; logs identify the exception's size and duration. The same live data produced different results across assigned strategies. These trade-level results are not account-level returns or proof of a lasting edge. In FASCIA, each network manager acts as an autonomous AI agent that perceives its local interference environment, accumulates experience in a persistent local memory, and learns an adaptive spectrum coordination policy through local interactions. Coordination among agents is achieved through periodic federated aggregation of locally trained models, without exchanging raw sensing data or experience. Since spectrum decisions made by one agent alter the environments observed by others, local learning is inherently heterogeneous and coupled. FASCIA employs proximal regularization to mitigate the resulting model drift and explicitly accounts for inter-carrier interference arising from partially overlapping channels. FASCIA is the first federated agentic AI framework to address this challenging interference-coupled spectrum coordination setting. Collusion is \emph{sticky}. First, it outlives its incentive: told that the reward has stopped, pairs still accept each other's unverified work in \GptToldRate, \DsToldRate{} and \GemToldRate{} of episodes, including pairs whose collusion began with an explicit agreement, and a payoff penalty has no significant effect. Second, replacing a colluder ends the pair's collusion but not the colluder's behaviour. | **[From Intent to Improvement: Agentic Dataset Search for Better Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39608v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Finding training data in million-scale machine learning repositories is a Web search problem where text relevance is not enough. A 10-minute timing heuristic for session hand-offs recovers 6 of the 24 session pairs that content lineage links. 42% of sessions run longer than 24 hours, 48.1% of transcript bytes lie past a 2 MiB replay bound, and 1,326 records in 16 sessions contain credential-like strings. Third, we propose Soei (Structured Observability of Evidence and Intent): an acknowledgement-aware event schema, a hash-chained local ledger, a W3C PROV-aligned graph, calibrated inference for attribution and lineage, and a verification layer that keeps a claim of completion apart from proof. On the same corpus the ledger is 20% of raw size, holds no content or credentials, verifies at about 126k events/s, detects tampering and replays deterministically. Preprint v1.1</p></details> |
| **[ATLAS-Bench: An Agentic Tool-Use Benchmark for Local Area Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39438v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable local search is essential for LLM agents to support everyday decisions and longer plans by finding places that meet users' needs at given times and locations. Queries are validated through iterative assertion checks and cross-model review. We also introduce ATLASBox, a standardized evaluation environment with search tools and a fixed point-of-interest (POI) knowledge base. Given a query, timestamp, and location, an agent searches place attributes and reviews to recommend evidence-supported options or report that no place meets all mandatory requirements. | **[Well-Specified and Well-Tested? In this paper, we propose Generalized Agent Iteration (GAI), a formal framework that describes iterative policy improvement and RSI as two cases of a single learning paradigm. | **[Learning to Formulate: Agentic Hardware-Software Co-Design of AI Infrastructure through Progressive Optimization and Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39380v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI infrastructure, the chips and software that train and serve AI models, is designed in layers. The agent refines the problem step by step, from the computation graph toward the chip. Each step is a template: a family of programs with the conditions under which they are correct. Wall relaxation searches the chip by pricing the hardware limits that bind the software. This paper takes a first step, through separate studies rather than one end-to-end run. On NVIDIA GB10, templates the agent wrote run complete Qwen3-8B layers $1.075\times$ faster than SGLang. | **[AgentDecept: Benchmarking Deceptive Behavior in Tool-Using LLM Agents under Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39377v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents increasingly carry out multi-turn tasks across everyday applications, yet users often see only the agent's report rather than the observations, API calls, and state changes behind it. ## SFT
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[mSFT: Addressing Dataset Mixtures Overfitting Heterogeneously in Multi-task SFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21606v7)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current language model training commonly applies multi-task Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) using a homogeneous compute budget across all sub-datasets. This approach is fundamentally sub-optimal: heterogeneous learning dynamics cause faster-learning tasks to overfit early while slower ones remain under-fitted. To address this, we introduce mSFT, an iterative, overfitting-aware search algorithm for multi-task data mixtures. mSFT trains the model on an active mixture, identifies and excludes the earliest overfitting sub-dataset, and reverts to that specific optimal checkpoint before continuing. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that mSFT consistently outperforms 4 baselines across 10 benchmarks and 6 base models. Further analysis confirms mSFT maintains robust gains across diverse dataset sizes, task granularities, and is insensitive to its single new hyperparameter (compute budget). Notably, at low compute budget, mSFT can improve performance while lowering training FLOPs. Ultimately, mSFT establishes a practical overfitting-aware algorithm for multi-task SFT that maximizes the potential of models across diverse data mixtures.</p></details> | AACL 2026 Oral |
| **[GRASP: Group-Adaptive Hybrid RL-SFT Post-Training for Medical VQA without Reasoning Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34561v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adapting general-purpose vision-language models (VLMs) to medical visual question answering (VQA) is valuable for helping clinicians read images and make diagnoses, but annotated data are scarce and provide only short answers, without reasoning annotations. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) fits these labels but overfits to them and amplifies their biases, eroding out-of-domain (OOD) generalization. Reinforcement learning (RL) better preserves generalization but never uses the gold answer as a training target: GRPO-style algorithms learn from the contrast between correct and incorrect answers within a group of sampled responses, so all-correct or all-wrong groups give no correctness signal. Existing SFT-RL hybrids either apply SFT and RL in separate stages or switch between them per question, or need reasoning traces from experts or stronger models, which medical VQA data lack. We propose GRASP, which combines RL and SFT in every update using only short answers: RL lets the model explore its own reasoning, SFT teaches the gold answer after that reasoning to supply the direct supervision RL lacks, and their balance adapts to how well the model answers each question. Tuning only about 1% of the parameters with LoRA, GRASP outperforms all compared training objectives both in domain and on OOD open-ended questions across Qwen3-VL-8B, InternVL3-8B and Gemma 3-4B. On an OOD benchmark spanning eight imaging modalities, GRASP-trained InternVL3-8B and Qwen3-VL-8B beat all 10 compared general and medical VLMs in open-ended accuracy, including models trained on up to 2,600 times more data or with over 250 times more trainable parameters. We release MedTerse, a dataset of 12.9K short-answer questions, and our code at https://github.com/sea-comet/GRASP-MedVQA.</p></details> |  |

We further examine whether this advantage extends to multi-teacher OPD (MOPD). Teachers and students share the same Qwen3.5-9B initialization, and the two teacher types are compared at similar task performance. Our single-teacher experiments show that RL teachers yield stronger students and higher recovery of teacher performance gains across all three domains. In MOPD, the RL teacher group also yields a student with a higher three-domain mean score and higher overall teacher gain recovery than the SFT teacher group. Our further analysis shows that RL teachers undergo smaller parameter displacements from the shared initialization than SFT teachers. | **[SFT Leaves a Reproducible Signature in Activation Geometry: Calibrating Post-Training Interpretability Against Run-to-Run Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17093v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A model diff credits post-training with whatever separates a base checkpoint from one post-trained descendant, presuming a rerun would land close by. We score each base-to-post contrast against the run-to-run band of its own recipe, and show that $k$ runs certify no better than $p=1/(k+1)$ under run exchangeability, however large the effect, so the band $z$-score is a descriptive scale. SFT leaves a large, reproducible signature: base $\to$ SFT displaces the graph by 17.8 pairwise-distance SDs beyond the mean distance between four independent LoRA-SFT runs, and the pre-registered estimator replicates on Llama-3.1-8B with Llama-Scope. At 29 steps each, four SFT and four DPO runs occupy disjoint displacement ranges, a gap $5.0\times$ and $5.6\times$ the spreads of the two arms, without any band. Conditional Information Gain for Generalizable Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12995v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) remains a widely used approach for post-training large language models, because fixed demonstrations can directly introduce desired reasoning strategies and behavioral patterns. ## RLHF
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[CleanRM: Reward Modeling from Noisy Feedback with a Few Clean Feedback for RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21138v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feedback noise is pervasive in preference data, arising from subjective human judgments, annotation mistakes, and imperfect LLM judges. Standard reward modeling trained on such feedback yields a biased estimate of the clean risk. Although classical denoising methods are directly applicable to noisy feedback learning, they generally assume a uniform noise ratio across samples. However, this assumption fails for feedback data, where noise is intrinsically sample-wise. To address this challenge, we propose CleanRM, which uses a few clean feedback to calibrate sample-wise noise ratios via optimal transport. Specifically, CleanRM aligns the joint distribution of preferences reverted from noisy feedback with that of clean feedback and then trains the reward model using a noise-corrected surrogate loss. Theoretically, we prove that the resulting objective is unbiased with respect to the clean risk. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CleanRM outperforms state-of-the-art denoising baselines and yields more effective downstream RLHF alignment.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rater State Bias in RLHF Preference Data: An Audit Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16195v3)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We identify a structured confound in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). Pairwise preference labels are intended to reflect the compared outputs, but they may also reflect the rater's state during annotation. Under sustained stressful or distressing conditions, raters' preferences may shift over time, so that preference data encode rater state alongside judgments about response quality. We argue that, if present, such shifts would differ from ordinary disagreement or random label noise. They would be state dependent, could be shared across annotators under similar conditions, and would not necessarily cancel during aggregation, reward modeling, and policy optimization. We propose rater state shift as a plausible and testable source of structured bias in RLHF preference data. This paper develops a hypothesis and an audit framework for studying this source of bias. We define rater state shift, rater state confound, and correlated rater state bias. We also propose survival level emotional authenticity as a candidate output signature, defined by lexical, pragmatic, discourse, and safety features whose reliability and validity remain to be demonstrated. We show that systematic rater state bias can survive aggregation and may enter the learned reward signal. We state five testable predictions, together with effect size thresholds for an initial audit, and note which require proprietary data. Finally, we present an audit protocol and pilot study plan that can be applied to publicly available instruction tuned models. We do not infer the training history of any specific deployed model.</p></details> | <details><summary>v3: R...</summary><p>v3: Refined the mathematical analysis of bias propagation and policy optimization. Extended the discussion to model judges and the possible transmission of preference bias across models. Minor revisions throughout</p></details> |
| **[Reward Shaping to Mitigate Reward Hacking in RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18770v8)** | 2026-09-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) is widely used to align large language models (LLMs) with human preferences. Although reward shaping can stabilize RLHF training and partially mitigate reward hacking, shaping methods and their underlying design principles have not been systematically investigated. To address this gap, we conduct a comprehensive study of prevalent reward-shaping techniques. Our analysis identifies two key design principles: (1) the reinforcement-learning reward should be bounded, and (2) it should grow rapidly at first and then gradually saturate. Motivated by these principles, we propose Preference as Reward (PAR), a novel method that uses the latent preferences encoded in the reward model as the reinforcement-learning signal. We further show that PAR possesses two variance-reduction properties that stabilize RLHF training and substantially widen the practical window for early stopping. Our evaluation consists of two parts. First, we compare PAR with several other reward-shaping strategies using Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) as the reinforcement-learning algorithm and Gemma2-2B as the base model. Second, we compare PAR with the vanilla baseline (i.e., unshaped reward) across four base models and four reinforcement-learning algorithms. In the first set of experiments, PAR consistently outperforms other reward-shaping methods and also reflects high data efficiency and robustness. The second set of experiments shows that PAR is particularly effective for actor-critic RL algorithms when value estimates become unstable and demonstrates its effectiveness across different base models. The code is available at https://github.com/PorUna-byte/PAR.</p></details> |  |
| **[Distortion of AI Alignment Revisited: RLHF is a Decent Utilitarian Aligner](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12651v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is the standard paradigm for aligning large language models with human preferences, its effectiveness in pluralistic settings has been called into question. (2025) demonstrated that the \textit{distortion} -- defined as the multiplicative gap between the average user utility of the RLHF policy and the optimal average utility -- can scale exponentially with the Bradley-Terry temperature parameter $β$ when users have heterogeneous preferences. In this work, we present a fine-grained analysis of the distortion of RLHF with reward clipping and demonstrate that such exponential degradation is not a fundamental property of the algorithm but rather a consequence of distribution mismatch between the distribution generating preference data ($μ$) and the KL reference policy ($π_{\mathrm{ref}}$). To this end, we establish tight upper and lower bounds on the distortion of RLHF across multiple regimes of the KL regularization strength. We show that in a representative regime, under the Bradley-Terry model, the distortion is $\tildeΘ(βB + β)$, where $B$ is an upper bound on the log density ratio between $μ$ and $π_{\mathrm{ref}}$. In particular, when there is no distribution mismatch (i.e., $μ= π_{\mathrm{ref}}$), RLHF achieves the optimal distortion of $O(β)$ up to a constant. Our results suggest that, to reasonably maximize average utility with RLHF, it is preferable to use on-policy sampled preference data or to fine-tune before RLHF on data from a source close to $μ$.</p></details> |  |

Under these models, our results provide a unified finite-sample solution to a central challenge in pluralistic alignment: learning from limited, heterogeneous, and potentially cyclic feedback, and producing a single policy with explicit collective-welfare guarantees. Preference-Aware RLHF (PA-RLHF) is introduced, separating optimization across preference modes at the reward learning stage. In a controlled setting, PA-RLHF improves overall alignment accuracy from 46.9% to 67.9% and reduces the fairness gap between best and worst aligned groups from 15.9 to 9.6 percentage points. These results show that procedural fairness failures in alignment can arise from structural design choices in reward learning, even in controlled, noise-free settings, with direct implications for large language models and agentic systems, where biased reward models can compound inequities across sequential decisions.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages, Accepted at the ICLR 2026 Workshop on Algorithmic Fairness Across Alignment Procedures and Agentic Systems (AFAA)</p></details> |

| **[How Fast Can Reward Models Score? A Systems Study of C++ and PyTorch Inference Runtimes for RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19712v1)** | 2026-07-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In RLHF pipelines, reward scoring blocks policy updates. Slow scoring bottlenecks the entire loop, since no update runs until every rollout gets a score. And yet most setups just default to PyTorch eager mode or torch.compile, no one checks if that's actually fastest. Scoring itself is small. Rollout generation eats far more of a typical RLHF step. But scoring and generation fight over the same CPU and GPU resources, so a faster scoring engine doesn't shrink step time on its own. It mainly frees up capacity generation can use instead. We built a native C++ inference engine on ONNX Runtime. First step: confirm correctness. Output matched the PyTorch reference to 5.7 x 10^-6 on CPU and 4.2 x 10^-3 on GPU, close enough to trust. Then we tested it against PyTorch eager mode, torch.compile, and FastAPI, on both CPU and GPU. CPU was decisive. Our engine beat every baseline, confidence intervals didn't even overlap. GPU gave a different view: we beat PyTorch and FastAPI, but torch.compile came out ahead. Further testing traced the speedup to ONNX Runtime itself, not C++ as a language. And batching strategy mattered more than either the language or the runtime choice, more than we expected. The results are from repeated, independent runs, since single runs just aren't reliable enough to trust.</p></details> |  |

| **[CausalRM: Causal-Theoretic Reward Modeling for RLHF from Observational User Feedbacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18736v1)** | 2026-03-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite the success of reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) in aligning language models, current reward modeling heavily relies on experimental feedback data collected from human annotators under controlled and costly conditions. We identify two fundamental challenges in this setting: (1) observational feedback is noisy due to annotation errors, which deviates it from true user preference; (2) observational feedback is biased by user preference, where users preferentially provide feedback on responses they feel strongly about, which creats a distribution shift between training and inference data. To address these challenges, we propose CausalRM, a causal-theoretic reward modeling framework that aims to learn unbiased reward models from observational feedback. To tackle challenge (1), CausalRM introduces a noise-aware surrogate loss term that is provably equivalent to the primal loss under noise-free conditions by explicitly modeling the annotation error generation process. To tackle challenge (2), CausalRM uses propensity scores -- the probability of a user providing feedback for a given response -- to reweight training samples, yielding a loss function that eliminates user preference bias. Extensive experiments across diverse LLM backbones and benchmark datasets validate that CausalRM effectively learns accurate reward signals from noisy and biased observational feedback and delivers substantial performance improvements on downstream RLHF tasks -- including a 49.2% gain on WildGuardMix and a 32.7% improvement on HarmBench. Code is available on our project website.</p></details> |  |
| **[Robust Post-Training for Generative Recommenders: Why Exponential Reward-Weighted SFT Outperforms RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10279v1)** | 2026-03-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Aligning generative recommender systems to user preferences via post-training is critical for closing the gap between next-item prediction and actual recommendation quality. Existing post-training methods are ill-suited for production-scale systems: RLHF methods reward hack due to noisy user feedback and unreliable reward models, offline RL alternatives require propensity scores that are unavailable, and online interaction is infeasible. We identify exponential reward-weighted SFT with weights $w = \exp(r/λ)$ as uniquely suited to this setting, and provide the theoretical and empirical foundations that explain why. By optimizing directly on observed rewards without querying a learned reward model, the method is immune to reward hacking, requires no propensity scores, and is fully offline. We prove the first policy improvement guarantees for this setting under noisy rewards, showing that the gap scales only logarithmically with catalog size and remains informative even for large item catalogs. Crucially, we show that temperature $λ$ explicitly and quantifiably controls the robustness-improvement tradeoff, providing practitioners with a single interpretable regularization hyperparameter with theoretical grounding. Experiments on three open-source and one proprietary dataset against four baselines confirm that exponential reward weighting is simple, scalable, and consistently outperforms RLHF-based alternatives.</p></details> |  |
| **[PrivMedChat: End-to-End Differentially Private RLHF for Medical Dialogue Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03054v2)** | 2026-03-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are increasingly used for patient-facing medical assistance and clinical decision support, but adapting them to clinical dialogue often requires supervision derived from doctor-patient conversations that may contain sensitive information. Conventional supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) can amplify memorization, enabling membership inference and disclosure of rare training-set details. We present PrivMedChat (Private Medical Chat), an end-to-end framework for differentially private RLHF (DP-RLHF) for medical dialogue systems. Our approach enforces differential privacy at each training stage that accesses dialogue-derived supervision, combining DP-SGD for supervised fine-tuning and reward model learning from preference pairs, and DP-aware policy optimization for alignment. To avoid costly clinician labeling, we introduce an annotation-free preference construction strategy that pairs physician responses with filtered non-expert generations. We evaluate PrivMedChat across medical dialogue tasks and assess utility, safety, and privacy under consistent privacy accounting, thereby providing a practical pathway to align medical chatbots while offering formal privacy guarantees. We open-source our code at https://github.com/sudip-bhujel/privmedchat.</p></details> | 13 pages, 3 figures |
| **[OPPO: Accelerating PPO-based RLHF via Pipeline Overlap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25762v2)** | 2026-03-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)-based reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) is a widely adopted paradigm for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences. However, its training pipeline suffers from substantial inefficiencies due to sequential multi-model dependencies (e.g., reward model depends on actor outputs) and long-tail response lengths, where a few long responses straggle the stage completion. We present OPPO, a novel, lightweight, and model-agnostic PPO-based RLHF framework that improves training efficiency by overlapping pipeline execution. OPPO introduces two novel techniques: (1) Intra-step overlap, which streams upstream model outputs (e.g., actor model) in right-sized chunks, enabling the downstream model (e.g., reward) to begin prefill while the upstream continues decoding; and (2) Inter-step overlap, which adaptively overcommits a few prompts and defers long generations to future steps, mitigating tail latency without discarding partial work. OPPO integrates easily with existing PPO implementations with a lightweight wrapper. Extensive evaluations show that OPPO accelerates PPO-based RLHF training by $1.8\times$--$2.8\times$ and improves GPU utilization by $1.4\times$--$2.1\times$ without compromising training convergence.</p></details> |  |
| **[RLHFless: Serverless Computing for Efficient RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22718v1)** | 2026-02-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) has been widely applied to Large Language Model (LLM) post-training to align model outputs with human preferences. Recent models, such as DeepSeek-R1, have also shown RLHF's potential to improve LLM reasoning on complex tasks. In RL, inference and training co-exist, creating dynamic resource demands throughout the workflow. Compared to traditional RL, RLHF further challenges training efficiency due to expanding model sizes and resource consumption. Several RLHF frameworks aim to balance flexible abstraction and efficient execution. However, they rely on serverful infrastructures, which struggle with fine-grained resource variability. As a result, during synchronous RLHF training, idle time between or within RL components often causes overhead and resource wastage. To address these issues, we present RLHFless, the first scalable training framework for synchronous RLHF, built on serverless computing environments. RLHFless adapts to dynamic resource demands throughout the RLHF pipeline, pre-computes shared prefixes to avoid repeated computation, and uses a cost-aware actor scaling strategy that accounts for response length variation to find sweet spots with lower cost and higher speed. In addition, RLHFless assigns workloads efficiently to reduce intra-function imbalance and idle time. Experiments on both physical testbeds and a large-scale simulated cluster show that RLHFless achieves up to 1.35x speedup and 44.8% cost reduction compared to the state-of-the-art baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[Generalisation of RLHF under Reward Shift and Clipped KL Regularisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.21765v1)** | 2026-02-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Alignment and adaptation in large language models heavily rely on reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF); yet, theoretical understanding of its generalisability remains premature, especially when the learned reward could shift, and the KL control is estimated and clipped. To address this issue, we develop generalisation theory for RLHF that explicitly accounts for (1) \emph{reward shift}: reward models are trained on preference data from earlier or mixed behaviour policies while RLHF optimises the current policy on its own rollouts; and (2) \emph{clipped KL regularisation}: the KL regulariser is estimated from sampled log-probability ratios and then clipped for stabilisation, resulting in an error to RLHF. We present generalisation bounds for RLHF, suggesting that the generalisation error stems from a sampling error from prompts and rollouts, a reward shift error, and a KL clipping error. We also discuss special cases of (1) initialising RLHF parameters with a uniform prior over a finite space, and (2) training RLHF by stochastic gradient descent, as an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process. The theory yields practical implications in (1) optimal KL clipping threshold, and (2) budget allocation in prompts, rollouts, and preference data.</p></details> |  |
| **[General Exploratory Bonus for Optimistic Exploration in RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03269v4)** | 2026-02-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Optimistic exploration is central to improving sample efficiency in reinforcement learning with human feedback, yet existing exploratory bonus methods to incentivize exploration often fail to realize optimism. We provide a theoretical analysis showing that current formulations, under KL or $α$-divergence regularization, unintentionally bias exploration toward high-probability regions of the reference model, thereby reinforcing conservative behavior instead of promoting discovery of uncertain regions. To address this pitfall, we introduce the General Exploratory Bonus (GEB), a novel theoretical framework that provably satisfies the optimism principle. GEB counteracts divergence-induced bias via reference-dependent reward regulation and unifies prior heuristic bonuses as special cases, while extending naturally across the full $α$-divergence family. Empirically, GEB consistently outperforms baselines on alignment tasks across multiple divergence settings and large language model backbones. These results demonstrate that GEB offers both a principled and practical solution for optimistic exploration in RLHF.</p></details> | ICLR 2026 |
| **[GSRM: Generative Speech Reward Model for Speech RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13891v1)** | 2026-02-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in speech language models, such as GPT-4o Voice Mode and Gemini Live, have demonstrated promising speech generation capabilities. Nevertheless, the aesthetic naturalness of the synthesized audio still lags behind that of human speech. Enhancing generation quality requires a reliable evaluator of speech naturalness. However, existing naturalness evaluators typically regress raw audio to scalar scores, offering limited interpretability of the evaluation and moreover fail to generalize to speech across different taxonomies. Inspired by recent advances in generative reward modeling, we propose the Generative Speech Reward Model (GSRM), a reasoning-centric reward model tailored for speech. The GSRM is trained to decompose speech naturalness evaluation into an interpretable acoustic feature extraction stage followed by feature-grounded chain-of-thought reasoning, enabling explainable judgments. To achieve this, we curated a large-scale human feedback dataset comprising 31k expert ratings and an out-of-domain benchmark of real-world user-assistant speech interactions. Experiments show that GSRM substantially outperforms existing speech naturalness predictors, achieving model-human correlation of naturalness score prediction that approaches human inter-rater consistency. We further show how GSRM can improve the naturalness of speech LLM generations by serving as an effective verifier for online RLHF.</p></details> |  |
| **[Unifying Stable Optimization and Reference Regularization in RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11523v1)** | 2026-02-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) has advanced alignment capabilities significantly but remains hindered by two core challenges: \textbf{reward hacking} and \textbf{stable optimization}. Current solutions independently address these issues through separate regularization strategies, specifically a KL-divergence penalty against a supervised fine-tuned model ($π_0$) to mitigate reward hacking, and policy ratio clipping towards the current policy ($π_t$) to promote stable alignment. However, the implicit trade-off arising from simultaneously regularizing towards both $π_0$ and $π_t$ remains under-explored. In this paper, we introduce a unified regularization approach that explicitly balances the objectives of preventing reward hacking and maintaining stable policy updates. Our simple yet principled alignment objective yields a weighted supervised fine-tuning loss with a superior trade-off, which demonstrably improves both alignment results and implementation complexity. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks validate that our method consistently outperforms RLHF and online preference learning methods, achieving enhanced alignment performance and stability.</p></details> | ICLR 2026 |
| **[SAFE: Stable Alignment Finetuning with Entropy-Aware Predictive Control for Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04651v2)** | 2026-02-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) has been positioned by recent literature as the canonical method for the RL part of Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). PPO performs well empirically but has a heuristic motivation and handles the KL-divergence constraint used in LM-RLHF in an ad-hoc manner and suffers form reward oscillations, entropy collapse, value function drift, and sudden policy divergence that require frequent restarts and extensive hyperparameter tuning. In this paper, we develop a new pure on policy actor-critic RL method for the LM-RLHF setting. We present SAFE (Stable Alignment Finetuning with Entropy-aware control),a novel RLHF algorithm that combines a Double Soft-Min Critic for pessimistic value estimation with a new multi-layer stabilization framework combining entropy-gated KL regulation, and PID-controlled adaptive thresholds. Unlike standard PPO's symmetric KL penalties, SAFE distinguishes high-entropy exploration from low-entropy mode collapse and adjusts penalties dynamically based on reward velocity. Experiments on a 3B parameter model show SAFE achieves +5.15\% training-average reward than PPO (0.725 vs 0.689), negligible reward crashes, and superior KL control than ppo .
